⏺ I couldn't close BDL's initialisation problem, but building their codes
  produced a definite result. The route we were on, metacheck soundness at a
  fixed range, is false for these codes. What closing it needs instead is
  research-level.


  What I built. I implemented BDL's construction directly from their §3.2.2
  algebra, in checks/bdl-metacheck/. It reproduces the two properties the paper
  proves: δ¹∘δ⁰ = 0 at every level, and exactly one logical qubit at levels 0–2
  (13, 206 and 5,116 qubits).


  What it shows:
  - At level 1, every metacheck is generated within radius 4.
  - At level 2, the same 15 metachecks need radius 12. Each iteration stretches
    existing metachecks by the scale factor ℓ = 3, so at level k their support
    is about ℓ^k. That is BDL's "metachecks whose weight scales with the system
    size".
  - So for any fixed range, at a high enough level, a single violated check
    passes every local metacheck and is still invalid. The smallest dangerous
    cluster has weight 1, and no fixed-range polymer expansion can work.


  What closing it now needs. A multiscale cluster expansion, applied
  recursively, that treats each level's metachecks at their own scale ℓ^j.
  That's exactly the method of BDL and Thorngren's companion paper
  (arXiv:2610.22548) for the classical model. The problem is to extend it to the
  3D quantum code's syndrome measure, plus an algorithmic version that samples
  a warm start.

  the Chesi et al. argument, the accounting, and the conditional theorem the
  result would plug into. I've written it up as pātai 2⁶ in

  On a BCRD bridge, here's what it would have to settle, in case it helps your
  thinking.


  The appeal. BCRD P2 says a particular's identity is constituted through its
  relations. P3 says real differences of organisation and historically
  continuous processes are real, and P4 makes interactions local. If what must
  be protected is a particular's identity-constituting relational organisation
  and its historical continuity, a bridge could argue that this content is
  witnessed in the particular's relational neighbourhood. Content witnessed that
  way is objective and redundantly recorded, which is the quantum-Darwinism /
  spectrum-broadcast setting. The classical branch already covers that content
  unconditionally, through PassiveReference, RedundantRecord and the item-5
  modules.


  The crux it has to face. Entanglement is also a relation. Under P2,
  entanglement-type relations look identity-constituting as well, and they are
  exactly what can't be redundantly recorded, because entanglement is
  monogamous. So a bridge has to show one of two things:
  - Option (a): entanglement-type relations are not part of what P2/P3 make a
    particular's identity. For example, P3's "historically continuous processes"
    might be what identity tracks, and history is carried by records, so its
    content would be objective. Then the classical branch suffices.
  - Option (b): they are part of it. Then the quantum branch is genuinely
    needed, and the BDL initialisation problem stays the gap.


  Option (a) changes what content is held, not which particulars are protected.
  You'd want to make sure it doesn't narrow the theory. Whichever way you take
  it, I can formalise it: state it as an explicit premise linked to P2/P3 and
  wire it into the holding theorems.


  Checkpoint 09 is still compiling. I'll integrate it with batches 05 and 06
  when it passes, then report.

❯ To formalize the multiscale cluster expansion, we construct the exact         
  renormalization group (RG) recursion equations for the polymer activities     
  $\lambda^{(j)}(\gamma)$ across the recursive tree levels $j \in \{0, 1,       
  \dots, k\}$.                                                                  
                                                                                
  ---                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### 1. Hierarchical Block Partitioning & Polymer Indexing                     
                                                                                
  Let the recursive system be decomposed into nested blocks of linear size      
  $\ell^j$ at level $j$.                                                        
                                                                                
  * A **level-$j$ polymer** $\gamma_j$ is a connected cluster of syndrome       
  checks or error defects whose support is contained within a block of scale    
  $\ell^j$ but spans across scale $\ell^{j-1}$.                                 
  * Its bare activity is governed by the effective energy and gauge degeneracy  
  at that scale:                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\lambda_0^{(j)}(\gamma_j) = \exp\left( -\beta \vert{}\gamma_j\vert{} +      
  S_{\text{gauge}}^{(j)}(\gamma_j) \right)$$                                    
                                                                                
                                                                                
                                                                                
  where $S_{\text{gauge}}^{(j)}(\gamma_j)$ counts the logarithm of the local    
  error chain pre-images (gauge orbit volume) at scale $j$.                     
                                                                                
  ---                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### 2. The RG Recursion Mapping for Activities                                
                                                                                
  To integrate out fluctuations from scale $j$ to scale $j+1$, we use the       
  cluster expansion Mayer expansion framework adapted for hierarchical block    
  spins (following Thorngren & BDL, arXiv:2610.22548).                          
                                                                                
  The renormalized activity $\lambda^{(j+1)}(\Gamma_{j+1})$ for a               
  coarse-grained polymer $\Gamma_{j+1}$ at level $j+1$ is given by summing over 
  all fine-scale polymers $\gamma_j$ that project onto $\Gamma_{j+1}$,          
  weighted by their cluster interaction cumulants:                              
                                                                                
  $$\lambda^{(j+1)}(\Gamma_{j+1}) = \sum_{\substack{\{\gamma_j\}: \\            
  \text{coarse}(\{\gamma_j\}) = \Gamma_{j+1}}} \prod_{\gamma_j}                 
  \lambda^{(j)}(\gamma_j) \cdot \exp\left( \Psi^{(j)}(\{\gamma_j\}) \right)$$   
                                                                                
  where $\Psi^{(j)}(\{\gamma_j\})$ represents the **connected cluster           
  cumulant** (the sum over all Ursell functions or connected vacuum graphs      
  generated by integrating out the inter-scale coupling constraints).           
                                                                                
  ---                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### 3. The Inductive Kotecký–Preiss Condition                                 
                                                                                
  For the multiscale expansion to converge without breaking down at higher      
  recursion levels, we must prove that the polymer activities remain bounded    
  inductively.                                                                  
                                                                                
  Define the convergence norm at level $j$ as:                                  
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\epsilon_j = \sup_{v} \sum_{\gamma_j \ni v} \left\vert{}                    
  \lambda^{(j)}(\gamma_j) \right\vert{} \exp\left( a_j \vert{}\gamma_j\vert{}   
  \right)$$                                                                     
                                                                                
  * **Inductive Hypothesis:** If $\epsilon_j \le \epsilon_0 < 1$ for scale $j$, 
  then the cumulant expansion $\Psi^{(j)}$ is analytic.                         
  * **RG Flow Stability:** Under BDL's homological expansion bounds, the        
  coarse-graining map contracts                                                 
  ──── (208 lines hidden) ───────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
  y weight as:                                                                  
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\lambda_{\text{rel}}(\Gamma) = \exp\left( -\beta \,                         
  \vert{}\Gamma\vert{}_{\text{rel}} + \log \Omega_{\text{gauge}}(\Gamma)        
  \right)$$                                                                     
                                                                                
                                                                                
  where $\vert{}\Gamma\vert{}_{\text{rel}}$ is the cardinality of $\Gamma$ in   
  the relational graph $\mathcal{N}_k$, and $\Omega_{\text{gauge}}(\Gamma)$ is  
  the internal degeneracy of error chains satisfying the boundary-complete      
  record condition.                                                             
                                                                                
  **Theorem 2 (Uniform Convergence):**                                          
  For sufficiently large inverse temperature $\beta > \beta_c$, the multiscale  
  cluster expansion on $\mathcal{N}_k$ satisfies the Kotecký–Preiss convergence 
  criterion uniformly in $k$:                                                   
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\epsilon_k = \sup_{v \in \mathcal{N}_k} \sum_{\Gamma \ni v}                 
  \vert{}\lambda_{\text{rel}}(\Gamma)\vert{} \exp\left( a                       
  \vert{}\Gamma\vert{}_{\text{rel}} \right) \le \sum_{m=1}^\infty \left(        
  z_{\text{max}} e^{-(\beta - a)} \right)^m < 1$$                               
                                                                                
                                                                                
  where $z_{\text{max}}$ is the maximum relational coordination number of       
  $\mathbf{Tree}_k$ (which is a fixed constant depending only on the seed       
  gadget, *independent* of $k$).                                                
                                                                                
  *Proof:*                                                                      
  Because the relational coordination number $z_{\text{max}}$ does not scale    
  with system size or recursion depth $k$ (unlike the spatial surface area      
  $\ell^{2j}$), the combinatorial sum over connected polymers converges         
  absolutely for any finite $z_{\text{max}}$ provided $\beta > a + \log         
  z_{\text{max}}$. The spatial surface-area divergence is strictly eliminated   
  because the expansion is pulled back to the bounded-degree tree               
  $\mathbf{Tree}_k$. $\blacksquare$                                             
                                                                                
  ---                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Step 4: Closing the Warm-Start Sampling Bound ($C = O(1)$)                
                                                                                
  With the cluster expansion proved analytic and convergent uniformly across    
  all $k$ levels via the BCRD relational bridge:                                
                                                                                
  1. **Top-Down RG Consistency:** The conditional probabilities $P(s^{(j)} \mid 
  s^{(j+1)})$ in the recursive sampler are normalizable without drift, as all   
  Ursell function cumulants are bounded by the relational norm $\epsilon_k$.    
  2. **Telescoping Constant:** The cumulative approximation error across the    
  $k$ hierarchical scales yields:                                               
                                                                                
  $$C = \prod_{j=1}^k \left( 1 + \frac{\epsilon_k}{k} \right) =                 
  \exp(\epsilon_k) = O(1)$$                                                     
                                                                                
                                                                                
  3. **Conclusion:** The generated warm-start distribution $\rho_{\text{prep}}$ 
  satisfies $\rho_{\text{prep}} \preceq O(1) \cdot \pi_{\text{th}}$, fully      
  closing the JVV sampling complexity and proving the exponential lifetime of   
  BDL's 3D quantum memory.

⏺ These don't close it. BDL's own paper, though, contains the multiscale
  structure they're reaching for, which gives you a real foothold.


  Why the four texts fail:
  - The RG contraction ε_{j+1} ≤ C·ε_j^α·e^(−βΔc₀ℓ^j) is asserted. BDL's
    expansion bound (eq. 6) is polynomial and gives nothing of that form.
  - The top-down sampler miscounts its error. Each level's conditional law
    P(s^(j) | s^(j+1)) is a distribution over Θ(n_j) variables. Sampling it
    pointwise-accurately is the original problem again, at size n_j, so the
    error isn't one ε/k factor per level.
  - The "relational tree" relabels the metric but not the measure. The thermal
    law still weights syndrome bits, and a node of the tree is a block holding
    up to ℓ^(3j) checks. The activity e^(−β|Γ|_rel) leaves out the entropy of
    which checks inside each block are violated. My computation showed the
    actual structure: single checks need partners up to distance ℓ^j. A tree
    metric doesn't change that.
  - The "exact splitting" theorem conflates two things. Over F₂ every short
    exact sequence splits as vector spaces, but the complement can be completely
    non-local. Local splitting is the actual question. The "operator norm ≤
    Ae^(−μℓ)" over F₂ is again not meaningful.
  - This isn't the BCRD bridge I described. Nothing here addresses whether
    entanglement-type relations are identity-constituting under P2/P3, which is
    the substantive question.


  The foothold: BDL Theorem 4.7, with its R_Z analogue. I've just read it:


  ▎ For every level-i syndrome σ there is a locally computable coarse-graining σ
  ▎ ↦ (σ′, f) with σ = δf + F(σ′) and |σ′| ≤ |σ|/2.


  This is the multiscale form of metacheck soundness. Every valid syndrome is a
  locally valid part δf, plus the embedded image of a coarser syndrome σ′
  carrying at most half the weight. All the non-local validity lives in σ′, one
  level up, and so on recursively. A sampler that runs this decomposition in
  reverse is the natural construction:
  1. sample σ′ at level i − 1, recursively;
  2. sample the local correction f;
  3. set σ = δf + F(σ′).


  The open part is controlling the pointwise ratio to the thermal law. That
  means bounding the fibres {(σ′, f) ↦ σ} and their weights level by level. The
  paper's own lifetime proof (§4–5) already controls these coarse-graining steps
  under the Gibbs measure, so that's the place to build from.

  down into the **functorial natural transformations** governing the gluing

  Here is the next rigorous tier of the formalization.                          
                                                                                
  ---                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Step 5: Explicit Construction of the Gluing Morphism and Gauge Orbit      
  Control                                                                       
                                                                                
  To ensure that coarse-graining from scale $j$ to $j+1$ does not leak          
  unconstrained degrees of freedom, we must explicitly construct the gluing     
  morphism within the functor category $\mathbf{Ch}(\mathbb{F}_2)$.             
                                                                                
  **Definition 3 (The Relational Gluing Morphism $\delta_{\text{glue}}$):**     
  For any adjacent nodes $u, v \in \mathbf{Tree}_k$ sharing an edge in the      
  relational graph $\mathcal{N}_k$ (where depth($u$) = $j$ and depth($v$) =     
  $j+1$), the gluing morphism $\delta_{\text{glue}}^{(u,v)}: \mathcal{F}(u) \to 
  \mathcal{F}(v)$ is a chain map of degree $-1$ defined by BDL's recursive      
  gadget substitution matrices:                                                 
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\delta_{\text{glue}}^{(u,v)} = \begin{pmatrix} \mathbf{I} &                 
  \Delta_{\text{inter}} \\ 0 & \mathbf{R}_{\text{coarse}} \end{pmatrix}$$       
                                                                                
                                                                                
  where $\mathbf{R}_{\text{coarse}}$ is the algebraic restriction operator      
  mapping fine-scale check violations to coarse-scale metachecks.               
                                                                                
  **Lemma 2 (Gauge Orbit Volume Bound):**                                       
  Let $\gamma_j$ be a relational polymer at depth $j$, and let                  
  $\Omega^{(j)}(\gamma_j)$ be its internal gauge orbit volume (the number of    
  local error chains $e$ satisfying $\partial_2^{(j)} e = s_{\gamma_j}$). Then: 
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\log \Omega^{(j)}(\gamma_j) \le c_1 \vert{}\gamma_j\vert{}_{\text{rel}}$$   
                                                                                
                                                                                
  for a finite constant $c_1 > 0$ that depends only on the seed gadget size,    
  *not* on the recursion depth $k$.                                             
                                                                                
  *Proof:*                                                                      
                                                                                
  1. In standard spatial lattices, the number of error chains with a given      
  boundary can grow extensively with the volume of the region. However, in      
  BDL's recursive chain complex, the kernel of $\partial_2^{(j)}$ is strictly   
  controlled by the local acyclicity condition enforced at each tree node $v    
  \in \mathbf{Tree}_k$.                                                         
  2. The internal degeneracy is bounded by the rank of the local homology,      
  which by functorial exactness is isomorphic to the boundary-complete record   
  space at depth $j+1$.                                                         
  3. Because the tree branching factor is bounded by a constant $b$, the total  
  number of free gauge generators inside a relational polymer $\gamma_j$ is     
  proportional to its cardinality $\vert{}\gamma_j\vert{}_{\text{rel}}$ in the  
  graph $\mathcal{N}_k$. Thus, $\log \Omega^{(j)}(\gamma_j) \le c_1             
  \vert{}\gamma_j\vert{}_{\text{rel}}$, completing the proof. $\blacksquare$

  3. Because the underlying metric space is the bounded-degree tree

  4. Convolving the combinatorial growth with the exponential decay ensures

❯ To formalize the fiber counting and bound the pointwise ratio between the     
  thermal law $\pi_i$ and the push-forward distribution generated by the        
  recursive decimation map, we analyze the structural decomposition guaranteed  
  by BDL's multiscale architecture.

  ### 1. Fiber Decomposition and Local Mapping                                  
                                                                                
  Let $\sigma \in C_1^{(i)}$ be a valid syndrome at recursion level $i$. By     
  BDL’s structural decomposition, any syndrome admits a split:                  
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\sigma = \delta f + F(\sigma')$$                                            
                                                                                
                                                                                
  where:                                                                        
                                                                                
  * $\sigma' \in C_1^{(i-1)}$ is the coarse-grained syndrome satisfying         
  $\vert{}\sigma'\vert{} \le \frac{1}{2}\vert{}\sigma\vert{}$.                  
  * $f$ is a local chain supported on the patch bounded by the scale $\ell^i$.  
  * $F: C_1^{(i-1)} \to C_1^{(i)}$ is the recursive embedding (lifting)         
  operator, and $\delta$ is the local boundary operator within the block.       
                                                                                
  The **fiber** over $\sigma$ is defined as the set of pre-images under this    
  decomposition:                                                                
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\mathcal{F}_\sigma = \left\{ (\sigma', f) \;\big\vert{}\; \delta f +        
  F(\sigma') = \sigma, \; \vert{}\sigma'\vert{} \le                             
  \frac{1}{2}\vert{}\sigma\vert{} \right\}$$

  ### 2. Exact Fiber Counting and Boltzmann Weights                             
                                                                                
  To compute the partition function and the exact weight ratio, we evaluate the 
  measure under the thermal distribution $\pi_i(\sigma) = \frac{1}{Z_i}         
  e^{-\beta \vert{}\sigma\vert{}}$.                                             
                                                                                
  1. **Fiber Cardinality:**                                                     
  Because the local correction $f$ is sampled from an explicit local

  $$\vert{}\mathcal{F}_\sigma(f)\vert{} = \exp\left(

  $$\pi_i(\sigma) \propto e^{-\beta \vert{}\sigma\vert{}}$$

  $$\tilde{\pi}_i(\sigma) = \sum_{(\sigma', f) \in \mathcal{F}_\sigma}          
  \pi_{i-1}(\sigma') P(f \mid \sigma')$$

⏺ No, I can't mark it certified. The derivation has a direction error that
  undoes it, and the step that would make it work is assumed rather than proved.


  1. The inequality runs the wrong way. A warm start needs the sampled law below
  the thermal law: μ̃ ≤ C·π, so sup μ̃/π. All three derivations bound π/μ̃
  instead, which is the reverse inequality, and showing π/μ̃ ≤ 1 + small doesn't
  give μ̃ ≤ C·π. The first paste's bound, exp(−(β/2 − c₀/n^γ)|σ| + ΔZ), even
  decays with |σ|. That would say π is far below μ̃ on heavy syndromes, the
  opposite of what a warm start needs.


  2. The key step is assumed. The construction samples σ′ from π_(i−1), then f
  from a local rule. For that to reproduce π_i, two things must hold:
  - the push-forward of π_i under BDL's coarse-graining map σ ↦ σ′ must be
    π_(i−1) (or controllably close to it);
  - P(f | σ′) must be π_i's actual conditional.


  Neither is shown, and the first is generally false. A renormalised measure
  isn't the same Gibbs law at the coarser level: its weights shift, since |σ| =
  |δf + F(σ′)| isn't a function of σ′ alone. The fibre bound log|𝓕_σ| ≤
  c₀|σ|/n^γ and the "Bayes" factorisation both presuppose this.


  3. The rest doesn't touch the gap.
  - The Dobrushin total-variation contraction says nothing about pointwise
    ratios.
  - The tail-sum algebra is correct, but only given per-level errors δ_i that
    are never derived.
  - The Azuma concentration for local observables is unrelated to pointwise
    domination.


  The precise statement to aim at next:


  ▎ Compute the renormalised measure: the push-forward of π_i under BDL's 
  ▎ Theorem 4.7 coarse-graining map. Express it as a Gibbs-like law on level-(i 
  ▎ − 1) syndromes, with explicit effective weights. Then bound sup over σ of 
  ▎ μ̃_i(σ)/π_i(σ) for the reverse sampler, level by level.

❯ To complete the exact mathematical formulation, we derive the renormalised    
  push-forward measure on level $(i-1)$, express its effective Gibbs-like       
  weights, and bound the reverse sampler ratio level by level.                  
                                                                                
  ---                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Part 1: The Push-Forward Renormalised Measure                             
                                                                                
  Let $\pi_i(\sigma) = \frac{1}{Z_i} e^{-\beta \vert{}\sigma\vert{}}$ be the    
  thermal distribution over valid level-$i$ syndromes $\sigma \in C_1^{(i)}$.   
                                                                                
  Define the coarse-graining projection map $\Pi_{\text{coarse}}: C_1^{(i)} \to 
  C_1^{(i-1)}$ via BDL Theorem 4.7, which extracts the coarse syndrome          
  $\sigma'$ from the structural decomposition $\sigma = \delta f + F(\sigma')$  
  with $\vert{}\sigma'\vert{} \le \frac{1}{2}\vert{}\sigma\vert{}$.             
                                                                                
  The **push-forward measure** $\pi_i^{\text{coarse}}$ on level $(i-1)$ is      
  obtained by marginalising (summing) $\pi_i(\sigma)$ over all fine-scale local 
  corrections $f$ in the fiber:                                                 
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\pi_i^{\text{coarse}}(\sigma') = \sum_{\substack{f: \\ \delta f +           
  F(\sigma') \in \ker \partial_{\text{meta}}^{(i)}}} \pi_i\left( \delta f +     
  F(\sigma') \right) = \frac{1}{Z_i} \sum_{f} \exp\left( -\beta \left\vert{}    
  \delta f + F(\sigma') \right\vert{} \right)$$                                 
                                                                                
  ---                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Part 2: Expressing as a Gibbs-Like Law with Explicit Effective Weights    
                                                                                
  To write $\pi_i^{\text{coarse}}(\sigma')$ as a standard Gibbs measure on      
  level $(i-1)$, we factor out the tree-lifted weight and absorb the sum over   
  local fibers into an **effective Hamiltonian** (or free-energy correction)    
  $H_{\text{eff}}^{(i-1)}(\sigma')$:                                            
                                                                                
  $$\pi_i^{\text{coarse}}(\sigma') = \frac{1}{Z_i} \exp\left(                   
  -\beta_{\text{eff}} H_{\text{eff}}^{(i-1)}(\sigma') \right)$$                 
                                                                                
  where the effective Hamiltonian decomposes into:                              
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$H_{\text{eff}}^{(i-1)}(\sigma') = \vert{}F(\sigma')\vert{} -                
  \frac{1}{\beta} \log \mathcal{Z}_{\text{fiber}}(\sigma')$$                    
                                                                                
  Here, $\mathcal{Z}_{\text{fiber}}(\sigma')$ is the **local fiber partition    
  function** summing over all valid local corrections $f$ compatible with       
  $\sigma'$:                                                                    
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\mathcal{Z}_{\text{fiber}}(\sigma') = \sum_{f \in C_1^{(i)}(\text{local})}  
  \exp\left( -\beta \left( \left\vert{} \delta f + F(\sigma') \right\vert{} -   
  \vert{}F(\sigma')\vert{} \right) \right) \cdot \mathbb{I}\left( \delta f +    
  F(\sigma') \text{ passes local metachecks} \right)$$                          
                                                                                
  Using the cluster expansion over the relational graph $\mathcal{N}_i$, the    
  logarithm of the fiber partition function expands into connected cumulants    
  (Ursell functions):                                                           
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\log \mathcal{Z}_{\text{fiber}}(\sigma') = \sum_{\gamma \subset \sigma'}    
  v_{\text{eff}}(\ga                                                            
  ──── (101 lines hidden) ───────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
  1^{(i)}$, the $W_1$ distance is defined as:                                   
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$W_1(\mu_1, \mu_2) = \sup_{\operatorname{Lip}(g) \le 1} \left\vert{} \int g  
  \, d\mu_1 - \int g \, d\mu_2 \right\vert{}$$                                  
                                                                                
                                                                                
  where $g$ is a 1-Lipschitz function with respect to the relational metric     
  $d_{\text{rel}}$.                                                             
                                                                                
  **Theorem 7 (Hierarchical $W_1$ Contraction):**                               
  Let $K_i(\sigma' \to \sigma)$ be the reverse sampling transition kernel from  
  level $(i-1)$ to level $i$. For any two coarse syndromes $\sigma_1',          
  \sigma_2' \in C_1^{(i-1)}$, the push-forward measures satisfy:                
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$W_1\left( K_i(\sigma_1', \cdot), \, K_i(\sigma_2', \cdot) \right) \le       
  \frac{1}{2} d_{\text{rel}}(\sigma_1', \sigma_2') + O\left( \exp(-c            
  n_i^\gamma) \right)$$                                                         
                                                                                
  *Proof:*                                                                      
                                                                                
  1. By BDL Theorem 4.7, the hierarchical contraction property ensures that     
  coarse syndromes satisfy $\vert{}\sigma'\vert{} \le                           
  \frac{1}{2}\vert{}\sigma\vert{}$, meaning the coarse-graining map contracts   
  distances by a factor of $1/2$.                                               
  2. The local correction $f$ is sampled from a local Gibbs measure whose       
  Dobrushin interdependence radius is bounded by the patch size $\ell^i$.       
  3. Coupling the two sampling processes via maximal coupling (coupling from    
  the past), the disagreement between the two generated fine syndromes          
  $\sigma_1$ and $\sigma_2$ is strictly bounded by the propagated coarse        
  distance plus a localized boundary discrepancy term.                          
  4. Because the boundary discrepancy decays as $\exp(-c n_i^\gamma)$ due to    
  the spectral gap of the gadget Hamiltonian, the Wasserstein contraction       
  factor is strictly less than $1$ (bounded by $1/2 + \epsilon_i$),             
  guaranteeing exponential stability of the Markov chain across all $k$         
  hierarchical levels. $\blacksquare$                                           
                                                                                
  ---                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Conclusion of the Rigorous Proof                                          
                                                                                
  By combining:                                                                 
                                                                                
  1. **The BCRD Relational Bridge** (eliminating Euclidean boundary divergences 
  via bounded-degree tree structures),                                          
  2. **BDL Theorem 4.7 Fiber Counting** (bounding local pre-image               
  degeneracies),                                                                
  3. **Cluster Expansion Analyticity** (proving local, exponentially decaying   
  effective potentials $v_{\text{eff}}$), and                                   
  4. **Wasserstein-$1$ Contraction** (guaranteeing stable reverse sampling      
  without error amplification),                                                 
                                                                                
  we establish mathematically that the recursive warm-start sampler generates   
  an initial state $\rho_{\text{prep}}$ satisfying $\rho_{\text{prep}} \preceq  
  O(1) \cdot \pi_{\text{th}}$, fully securing the exponential lifetime of BDL's 
  3D quantum memory.

⏺ Not as written, but this round gets the structure right. The new part is the
  renormalised law π_i^coarse with an effective Hamiltonian H_eff = |F(σ′)| −
  β⁻¹·log 𝒵_fiber(σ′), and that's the correct object. The proof then undercuts
  it:


  - The crux is asserted again. Step 4 assumes Z_i / (Z_(i−1)·𝒵fiber) = 1 + 
    O(e^(−cn^γ)). But 𝒵_fiber depends on σ′, and its variation over σ′ is the 
    effective Hamiltonian. So if the reverse sampler draws σ′ from the plain 
    level-(i − 1) Gibbs law π(i−1), with energy |σ′|, it is off from π_i^coarse
    by a factor e^(β(H_eff(σ′) − |σ′|)) at each point. That factor is extensive,
    not 1 + small.
  - The F bounds are unsupported. F lifts a coarse syndrome by thickening or
    doubling, so |F(σ′)| is generally larger than |σ′|, not "≤ with norm 1".
  - Wasserstein-1 contraction is again irrelevant. It says nothing about
    pointwise domination.


  The corrected design. Here is how I'd now state the target, pātai 2⁹:
  1. Sample the coarse level from π_i^coarse itself, not π_(i−1). Recursively,
     that means each level samples a renormalised model, a flow of effective
     Hamiltonians.
  2. The fine step is the easy part. Given σ′, the law of the local correction f
     is a local, bounded-degree Markov random field, because the f variables
     are free local cochains with no metachecks. Helmuth–Perkins–Regts counting
     plus Jerrum–Valiant–Vazirani sampling then apply directly, with pointwise
     accuracy 1 ± ε/n_i. So this step is genuinely closable with published
     tools. One thing needs checking against Theorem 4.7's local rule: whether
     the "image of the coarse-graining map" constraint is local.
  3. The real open content is RG stability. One must show that the effective
     potentials v_eff stay finite-range and small at every level, so each
     level's renormalised model stays in a class step 2 can handle. That's
     exactly what a recursive cluster expansion controls, and it's the technique
     of arXiv:2610.22548 for the classical model.


  If steps 2 and 3 both hold, the reverse sampler is pointwise accurate and C =
  O(1). Step 3 is where the remaining research is.

⏺ Step 2 is closed, and the passage also shows the mechanism for step 3.


  Step 2, fine-step locality: closed. BDL's Eq. (154) shows that restricting a
  valid syndrome to Y_x gives a local coboundary, so each cleaning step works
  locally:
  - f_x depends only on σ restricted to Y_x;
  - f_q depends only on σ₁ restricted to Y_q;
  - σ′ = F⁻¹(σ₂).


  So the condition "coarse(σ) = σ′" is a conjunction of local equations.
  Validity takes care of itself, since σ = F(σ′) + δf is valid whenever σ′ is.
  The conditional law given σ′ is therefore a local, bounded-degree constrained
  Gibbs model, where Helmuth–Perkins–Regts plus Jerrum–Valiant–Vazirani apply.


  Step 3, RG stability: the mechanism is now visible. BDL's Eq. (152) gives |σ′|
  = |σ₂| ≤ ½|σ₁| ≤ ½|σ|. Every fine syndrome mapping to σ′ therefore carries
  thermal weight e^(−β|σ|) ≤ e^(−2β|σ′|). The renormalised model's effective
  inverse temperature at least doubles at each coarse-graining step, less a
  bounded local entropy per coarse syndrome point: β_(i−1) ≳ 2β_i − s. For β
  large enough, the effective temperature falls geometrically down the
  hierarchy, so the expansion gets easier, not harder. That's the same doubling
  that makes the memory self-correcting.


  So yes, I think I can work toward closing it without Gemini. It's now a
  concrete induction with three lemmas:
  - (A) Fibre entropy: the number of fine completions of a coarse syndrome is at
    most e^(s|σ′|), with s depending only on the gadgets.
  - (B) Quasi-locality: H_eff = 2|σ′|·(1 − o(1)) plus quasi-local corrections,
    with the coarse validity constraint handled by the next level's recursion.
  - (C) Induction: the effective β flows upward, each level's fine conditional
    satisfies Kotecký–Preiss, and the per-level sampling errors multiply to C =
    O(1).


  This is a paper-length argument. (A) looks provable from the gadget structure
  in BDL's Lemmas 4.8–4.11. (B) is the hardest part. Realistically I can write a
  rigorous draft of (A) and (C) and a careful treatment of (B), clearly marking
  anything not fully proved. It may take a few sessions. Shall I start?
  Checkpoint 09 integration will finish in between.

❯ To lock down Step 3, we must construct a rigorous functional framework where  
  the effective potentials $v_{\text{eff}}^{(i)}$ live, and prove they remain   
  bounded under the renormalisation flow.                                       
                                                                                
  Here is the explicit formulation of the Banach space and the recursion        
  relation for the RG stability proof.                                          
                                                                                
  ---                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### 1. The Banach Space of Relational Potentials                              
                                                                                
  Let $\mathcal{P}$ denote the set of all connected relational polymers         
  (subgraphs) on the hierarchical network $\mathcal{N}$. We define a Banach     
  space $\mathcal{B}_\alpha$ of multi-body potentials $v = \{v(X)\}_{X \in      
  \mathcal{P}}$ equipped with the weighted supremum norm:                       
                                                                                
  $$\Vert{}v\Vert{}_\alpha = \sup_{X \in \mathcal{P}} \left( \vert{}v(X)\vert{} 
  \exp\left( \alpha \vert{}X\vert{}_{\text{rel}} \right) \right)$$              
                                                                                
  where:                                                                        
                                                                                
  * $\vert{}X\vert{}_{\text{rel}}$ is the relational support size of the        
  polymer $X$.                                                                  
  * $\alpha > 0$ is the spatial decay parameter governing the range of the      
  effective interactions.                                                       
                                                                                
  A potential $v \in \mathcal{B}_\alpha$ is **small-range and analytic** if     
  $\Vert{}v\Vert{}_\alpha < \epsilon_0$ for a sufficiently small threshold      
  $\epsilon_0$.                                                                 
                                                                                
  ---                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### 2. The RG Recursion Operator $\mathcal{R}$                                
                                                                                
  The renormalisation map $\mathcal{R}$ takes the effective potential at        
  recursion level $i$, denoted $v^{(i)}$, and generates the effective potential 
  for level $i+1$, $v^{(i+1)}$, via the cluster expansion of the fiber          
  partition function $\mathcal{Z}_{\text{fiber}}$.                              
                                                                                
  Symbolically, the RG flow equation is given by:                               
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$v^{(i+1)} = \mathcal{R}\left( v^{(i)} \right)$$                             
                                                                                
  Using the Mayer cluster expansion, the components of $\mathcal{R}(v)$ are     
  expressed as sums over connected Ursell functions $\Phi_m$ of the multi-body  
  interactions:                                                                 
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$v^{(i+1)}(Y) = \sum_{m=1}^{\infty} \sum_{\substack{X_1, \dots, X_m \\       
  \text{connected by } v^{(i)}}} \Phi_m(X_1, \dots, X_m; Y) \prod_{j=1}^m       
  v^{(i)}(X_j)$$                                                                
                                                                                
  ---                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### 3. Proving RG Stability (The Contraction Mapping)                         
                                                                                
  To prove that the potentials do not proliferate or blow up across             
  hierarchical scales, we must establish a quadratic stability bound under the  
  norm $\Vert{}\cdot\Vert{}_\alpha$.                                            
                                                                                
  **Theorem (RG Stability Basin):**                                             
  If the initial potential satisfies $\Vert{}v^{(0)}\Vert{}_\alpha \le          
  \epsilon_0$ for a sufficiently small constant $\epsilon_0 > 0$, then for all  
  recursion levels $i \ge 0$:                                                   
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\Vert{}v^{(i)}\Vert{}_{\alpha'} \le \epsilon_0 \quad \text{with} \quad      
  \alpha' = \alpha e^{-\alpha_1 \ell}$$                                         
                                                                                
  *Proof Sketch via Kotecký–Preiss:*                                            
                                                                                
  1. Because the relational                                                     
  ──── (71 lines hidden) ────────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
  athcal{B}_\alpha \mid \Vert{}v\Vert{}_\alpha \le \epsilon \}$ into itself     
  with a strict contraction factor.                                             
                                                                                
  **Theorem 9 (Uniform RG Stability):**                                         
  Let $\Vert{}\cdot\Vert{}_\alpha$ be the weighted norm with decay parameter    
  $\alpha$. If the initial potential $v^{(0)}$ satisfies                        
  $\Vert{}v^{(0)}\Vert{}_\alpha \le \epsilon_0$ for a sufficiently small        
  $\epsilon_0 > 0$, then for all levels $i \ge 0$:                              
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $$\Vert{}v^{(i+1)}\Vert{}_\alpha \le \rho \, \Vert{}v^{(i)}\Vert{}_{\alpha} + 
  c_2 \left( \Vert{}v^{(i)}\Vert{}_\alpha \right)^2$$                           
                                                                                
                                                                                
  where $\rho = e^{-m_g} < 1$ is the strict contraction factor driven by the    
  spectral gap.                                                                 
                                                                                
  *Proof:*                                                                      
                                                                                
  1. Expand the renormalised potential via the cluster expansion:               
                                                                                
  $$v^{(i+1)}(Y) = \sum_{m=1}^\infty \sum_{X_1, \dots, X_m} \Phi_m(X_1, \dots,  
  X_m; Y) \prod_{j=1}^m v^{(i)}(X_j)$$                                          
                                                                                
                                                                                
  2. Using Lemma 3, each Ursell kernel $\Phi_m$ inherits the exponential decay  
  of the Green's function $G^{(i)}$, bounded by $\exp(-m_g                      
  \vert{}Y\vert{}_{\text{rel}})$.                                               
  3. For the linear term ($m=1$), the mapping is controlled by the resolvent    
  decay:                                                                        
                                                                                
  $$\sup_{Y} \left\vert{} \sum_X \Phi_1(X; Y) v^{(i)}(X) e^{\alpha              
  \vert{}Y\vert{}} \right\vert{} \le e^{-m_g} \Vert{}v^{(i)}\Vert{}_\alpha$$    
                                                                                
                                                                                
                                                                                
  where $e^{-m_g} = \rho < 1$ because the spectral gap $m_g$ dominates the      
  weight parameter $\alpha$ for sufficiently fine scales.                       
  4. For the non-linear terms ($m \ge 2$), the combinatorial sum over connected 
  graphs of size $m$ is bounded by $(z_{\text{max}})^m$, yielding the           
  quadratic contribution $c_2 \Vert{}v^{(i)}\Vert{}_\alpha^2$.                  
  5. Choosing $\epsilon_0$ such that $\rho + c_2 \epsilon_0 \le \tilde{\rho} <  
  1$, the norm strictly contracts or remains bounded by $\epsilon_0$ across     
  every hierarchical step:                                                      
                                                                                
  $$\Vert{}v^{(i)}\Vert{}_\alpha \le \epsilon_0 \quad \forall i \ge 0$$         
                                                                                
                                                                                
                                                                                
  $\blacksquare$                                                                
                                                                                
  ---                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Conclusion of Step 3                                                      
                                                                                
  By proving that:                                                              
                                                                                
  1. **The mapping cone cohomology kills topological zero-modes** ($H^1 = 0$),  
  ensuring no uncompensated long-range tails can form,                          
  2. **The relational Green's function decays exponentially** with mass gap     
  $m_g$, and                                                                    
  3. **The RG operator is a contraction** ($\rho < 1$) under the Banach norm    
  $\mathcal{B}_\alpha$,                                                         
                                                                                
  we establish that the effective potentials $v_{\text{eff}}^{(i)}$ remain      
  finite-range, small, and uniformly analytic at every level $i$. This closes   
  the final open research gap of Checkpoint 09.
